**2(f) — 3 marks.** In 2024 it was recommended that additional funding be provided for drainage of farmland in India. Explain one way improved drainage may affect the productivity of Indian farmers.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q2 PDF pages 7–11; prompt match p. 9 (prompt-prefix-8-words); MS PDF p. 10
- **Context locator:** Exact prompt and relevant stimulus manually transcribed/checked against visually reviewed QP printed p. 9; see local official QP.

- **MS:** AO2 3 marks Award 1 mark for identifying a relevant way Award 1 mark for developing the way Award 1 mark for the response being in context Drainage would reduce flooded areas of land (1) meaning farmers could grow more crops on the newly available land (1) increasing the output/leading to higher productivity (1) Accept any other appropriate response (3)

**2(g) — 9 marks.** In 2024, Kuwait’s minimum wage was 75 KWD (about $243) per month. With reference to this data and your knowledge of economics, assess the likely effect of having a minimum wage on the demand for labour in Kuwait.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q2 PDF pages 7–11; MS PDF p. 11
- **Context locator:** Exact prompt and relevant stimulus manually transcribed/checked against visually reviewed QP printed p. 10; see local official QP.

- **MS:** AO2 (3 marks)/AO3 (3 marks)/AO4 (3 marks) AO2 and AO3  Having a minimum wage means a higher income is received by people who may have struggled with basic needs than if the national minimum wage was not in place  A minimum wage may affect the demand for labour because firms may need to pay their workers more in exchange for the work carried out  Therefore, it could increase the costs of the firms and consequently, may reduce profit  Possible use of a diagram:  As the wage rate increased from W1 to the minimum wage (75 KWD) it means that fewer workers may be employed by the firms, so the quantity of labour demanded may decrease from Q1 to Q2 AO4  However, some firms may already have been paying its workers above 75 KWD per month  Therefore, the legislation would not cause a change in the costs of those firms and consequently it may not affect their profits  This may mean that the demand for labour is unchanged  Despite this, the introduction of a minimum wage may put pressure on firms to maintain a higher wage rate to retain workers, meaning demand for labour may still decrease  It may depend on the number of firms paying at or above the minimum wage before its introduction Level Mark Descriptor No rewardable material. Level 1–3  Demonstrates basic knowledge and understanding by developing relevant points. Limited application of economic terms, concepts, theories and calculations (AO2).  Information presented will lack selectivity and organisation. Interpretation of economic information will be limited, with a lack of analysis of issues (AO3).  Only offers one viewpoint, meaning there is an unbalanced and incomplete evaluation, showing limited understanding and awareness (AO4). Level 4–6  Demonstrates partial knowledge and understanding by developing relevant points. Partial application of economic terms, concepts, theories and calculations (AO2).  Information presented will demonstrate some selectivity and organisation. Interpretation of economic information will be good, with some analysis of issues (AO3).  Offers more than one viewpoint but the argument may lack balance, leading to an evaluation that may not demonstrate full understanding and awareness (AO4). Level 7–9  Demonstrates clear knowledge and understanding by developing relevant points. Appropriate application of economic terms, concepts, theories and calculations (AO2).  Information presented will demonstrate excellent selectivity and organisation. Interpretation of economic information will be excellent, with a thorough analysis of issues (AO3).  Offers more than one viewpoint. The argument is well balanced and coherent, leading to an evaluation that demonstrates full understanding and awareness (AO4).

**3(d) — 6 marks.** Olive-oil prices peaked at €10,000 per tonne in summer 2024 and are expected to fall to €4,000–€5,000 per tonne in 2025. After years of heatwaves and droughts, Greece, Spain and Tunisia expect better olive harvests. Greece’s expected production rises from 130,000 to 230,000 tonnes; Spain and Tunisia also forecast increases. With reference to this data and your knowledge of economics, analyse how natural factors such as favourable weather may affect the olive-oil market in 2025.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q3 PDF pages 12–16; prompt match p. 24 (prompt-prefix-3-words); MS PDF p. 15

- **MS:** AO2 (3 marks)/AO3 (3 marks) AO2 and AO3  Possible use of a diagram:  Natural factors, such as favourable weather could shift the supply curve outwards/to the right (from S to S1)  Favourable weather can increase supply because it can make it easier to grow olives and therefore make it cheaper to produce olive oil  Greece’s production was expected to increase by 100,000 tonnes from the previous year in the 2025 season (Qe to Q1)  This will therefore result in a decrease in the price (from Pe to P1) if demand stays the same  This is evident from the expected fall in price of olive oil by at least 50%, from €10 000 per tonne to between €4 000 and €5 000 per tonne Level Mark Descriptor No rewardable material. **3(e) — 9 marks.** Fitnation is a fitness centre in Almaty, Kazakhstan, offering activities including boxing. It aims to do charitable work by giving city residents access to boxing facilities and coaching at a discounted price one day a week, making it more accessible to people who otherwise could not afford it. However, its facilities are very busy that day, reducing availability for members paying the full monthly price of $25–$50. With reference to this data and your knowledge of economics, assess the benefits to Fitnation of offering the discounted price.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q3 PDF pages 12–16; prompt match p. 15 (prompt-prefix-8-words); MS PDF p. 17
- **Context locator:** Exact prompt and relevant stimulus manually transcribed/checked against visually reviewed QP printed p. 15; see local official QP.

- **MS:** AO2 (3 marks)/AO3 (3 marks)/AO4 (3 marks) AO2 and AO3  Fitnation may have aims that include maximising profit, caring for customers, completing charitable work or maximising revenue/sales  As Fitnation regularly offers discounted entry to city residents, making a profit is not its main aim  Instead, the aim could be described as completing charitable work, meaning the centre is contributing towards benefits for the country  Some people may visit the fitness centre because they like the aim of the business, therefore increasing custom  As a result, the profit of Fitnation may increase as well as an increase in sales due to it completing charitable work AO4  However, as fewer facilities are available for members one day a week, some may decide that paying a fee/price of between $25 and $50 is not in their best interests  This is because they cannot make the most of their membership and the money they paid, so they are not able to maximise their benefit  Therefore, the number of members/people paying full price to access the facilities at the fitness centre may decrease  This means that revenue may fall and it may be more difficult for Fitnation to make a profit Level Mark Descriptor No rewardable material. **4(b) — 6 marks.** Bulgaria has an extensive rail network and low-priced trains and buses, but rail services have frequent cancellations and poor safety while buses are often late and much slower than private cars. With reference to this data and your knowledge of economics, analyse how income elasticity of demand (YED) may affect public-transport operators as incomes increase in Bulgaria.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q4 PDF pages 17–22; prompt match p. 18 (prompt-prefix-5-words); MS PDF p. 20
- **Context locator:** Exact prompt and relevant stimulus manually transcribed/checked against visually reviewed QP printed p. 18; see local official QP.

- **MS:** AO2 (3 marks)/AO3 (3 marks) AO2 and AO3  By calculating YED, operators of public transport would be able to see the likely effect on demand following an increase in income  Owing to the poor standard of rail travel and of buses often being late in Bulgaria, public transport may be an inferior good  Inferior goods have a negative income elasticity of demand, meaning as incomes increase, demand decreases  Therefore, an increase in income in Bulgaria is likely to lead to a fall in demand for bus and train travel as they switch to using private cars  This means operators of public transport would lose custom due to incomes increasing  Knowledge of YED would allow the operators to take action to improve quality/service according to the likely outcome of YED calculations Level Mark Descriptor No rewardable material. Partial application of economic terms, concepts, theories and calculations (AO2).  Information presented will demonstrate some selectivity and organisation. Interpretation of economic information will be good, with some analysis of issues (AO3). Level 5–6  Demonstrates clear knowledge and understanding by developing relevant points. Appropriate application of economic terms, concepts, theories and calculations (AO2).  Information presented will demonstrate excellent selectivity and organisation. Interpretation of economic information will be excellent, with a thorough analysis of issues (AO3).

**4(c) — 12 marks.** In January 2025, Western Australia announced AU$107m investment to expand the 72 km Swan River ferry network. Five new electric ferries, built by local firms, can carry over 130 passengers each; two stops would connect to a bus depot and the University of Western Australia. Local sailing clubs raised safety concerns, while opponents warned extra ferry-user parking could cause problems. With reference to this data and your knowledge of economics, evaluate the extent to which public-sector investment may benefit Western Australia’s economy.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q4 PDF pages 17–22; prompt match p. 20 (prompt-prefix-3-words); MS PDF p. 21
- **Context locator:** Exact prompt and relevant stimulus manually transcribed/checked against visually reviewed QP printed p. 20; see local official QP.

- **MS:** AO2 (4 marks)/AO3 (4 marks)/AO4 (4 marks) AO2 and AO3  The public sector is a part of the economy which is owned by the government and operates in the public interest  An investment of AU$107m would greatly improve the Swan River ferry network, by increasing the number of ferries and stops along the river  This means there would be greater capacity to transport passengers (>130 people per ferry trip), connecting more people across the Perth area  Therefore, it may be easier/more accessible for people to work in more locations in Perth than without the increase in infrastructure  A greater number and variety of jobs is likely to be available both during the building and operation of the additional ferries  This would create a positive effect for local firms and may increase more revenue for the Western Australia Government through tax  Instead of causing a parking issue, the investment may reduce traffic, as the new stops would allow passengers to travel more easily by buses connecting with the ferries and/or access to university without using private transport AO4  However, the cost of expanding the ferry network means the Western Australian Government would have less revenue available for spending on health and education  Therefore, the economy of Western Australia may not benefit as much from public sector investment to improve the network as if the AU$107m was invested elsewhere  This may be intensified if parking by ferry users leads to accidents and/or congestion due to the increased usage  It will most likely take time for the building of new ferries and addition of new stops, meaning any benefit may not be seen for a number of years  Therefore, costs may be considerably higher than revenue, for the government and the economy during this time  Safety concerns have been raised, meaning additional spending may be needed to investigate potential dangers to small boat users and to rectify problems Level Mark Descriptor No rewardable material. Level 1 1–4  Demonstrates isolated elements of relevant knowledge and understanding; may lack development of relevant points. Limited application of economic terms, concepts, theories and calculations (AO2).  Information presented will lack selectivity and organisation. Chain of reasoning may be attempted. Interpretation of economic information will be limited, with a lack of analysis of issues (AO3).  Only offers one viewpoint, meaning there is an unbalanced and incomplete evaluation, showing limited understanding and awareness. A judgement or conclusion is unlikely to be attempted (AO4). Level 2 5–8  Demonstrates partial selectivity and some accurate knowledge and understanding by developing some relevant points. Partial application of economic terms, concepts, theories and calculations (AO2).  Information presented will demonstrate some selectivity and organisation. Chain of reasoning will be present but may be limited. Interpretation of economic information will be good, with some analysis of issues (AO3).  Offers more than one viewpoint but the argument may lack balance, leading to an evaluation that may not demonstrate full understanding and awareness. A judgement or conclusion is attempted, but may not be fully supported (AO4). Level 3 9–12  Demonstrates specific and accurate knowledge and understanding by developing relevant points. Appropriate application of economic terms, concepts, theories and calculations (AO2).  Information presented will demonstrate excellent selectivity and organisation. Chain of reasoning will be coherent and logical. Interpretation of economic information will be excellent with a thorough analysis of issues (AO3).  Offers more than one viewpoint. The argument is well balanced and coherent, leading to an evaluation that demonstrates full understanding and awareness. A supported judgement or conclusion is present (AO4).


## January 2023 — P1R  (80 marks)

QP: `uploads/Incoming_Economics_2026-11/4ec1-01r-que-20230114.pdf` · MS: [official Pearson MS](https://qualifications.pearson.com/content/dam/pdf/International%20GCSE/Economics/2017/exam-materials/4ec1-01r-rms-20230302.pdf)

> Source note: Official Pearson January 2023 Paper 01R. QP log P71556A. MS cover names 01R but its printed Publications Code is 4EC1_01_MS_2301 (without R); see Source_Notes/Examiner_Reports/2023-01_P1R_Source_Audit.md. Indicative content is summarized or paraphrased from Pearson’s MS; level descriptor tables are summarized.

**1(a) — 1 marks.** Water is an example of which factor of production? A Land; B Labour; C Capital; D Enterprise.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q1 PDF pages 2–6; prompt match p. 2 (prompt-prefix-8-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.2.

- **MS:** AO1 1 mark. The only correct answer is A, Land. B is labour (a human resource); C is capital (a man-made resource); D is enterprise (the element of risk).

**1(b) — 1 marks.** A firm has total costs of $700 and sells 300 items at $20 each. What is its profit? A $5,300; B $6,000; C $13,700; D $14,000.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q1 PDF pages 2–6; prompt match p. 2 (prompt-prefix-5-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.2; sales revenue and total costs are given.

- **MS:** AO2 1 mark. The only correct answer is A, $5,300 ($700 total costs subtracted from $6,000 revenue).

**1(c) — 2 marks.** What is meant by the term supply?
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q1 PDF pages 2–6
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.2.

- **MS:** AO1 2 marks. Award 1 mark for the amount producers are willing and able to sell and 1 mark for reference to price. The quantity of goods or services producers are willing and able to sell at a given price or time. Accept any other appropriate response.

**1(d) — 1 marks.** State one type of external economy of scale.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q1 PDF pages 2–6; prompt match p. 3 (prompt-prefix-3-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.3.

- **MS:** AO1 1 mark. Any one correct type of external economy of scale: skilled labour, infrastructure, access to suppliers, or similar businesses in the area. Accept any other appropriate response.

**1(e) — 1 marks.** Define the term unique product.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q1 PDF pages 2–6; prompt match p. 3 (prompt-prefix-5-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.3.

- **MS:** AO1 1 mark. A good or service for which there is no substitute. Accept any other appropriate response.

**1(f) — 2 marks.** Connor owns a hair salon. He increased prices by 2% and was willing to increase quantity supplied by 2.6%. Calculate the price elasticity of supply (PES) for Connor’s hair salon. Show your working.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q1 PDF pages 2–6; prompt match p. 3 (prompt-prefix-12-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.3.

- **MS:** AO2 2 marks. Correct calculation: 2.6% ÷ 2% = 1.3. Award one mark for the calculation and one for the correct PES; award both for 1.3 even without workings. Do not award marks for a formula alone.

**1(g) — 3 marks.** Using the supplied diagram, draw the likely effect on the market for luxury holidays after a fall in income. Label the new curve, new equilibrium price and new equilibrium quantity.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q1 PDF pages 2–6
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.4; luxury holidays are the good, so the income change is the relevant demand determinant.

- **MS:** AO2 3 marks. Award 1 mark for a labelled leftward shift of demand, 1 for the labelled lower equilibrium price, and 1 for the labelled lower equilibrium quantity.

**1(h) — 3 marks.** Government-owned Angola Telecom is to be privatised in an attempt to make the industry more efficient. Explain one possible disadvantage of privatisation for workers in Angola’s telecoms industry.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q1 PDF pages 2–6; prompt match p. 4 (prompt-prefix-12-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.4; Angola Telecom’s planned privatisation and efficiency objective provide context.

- **MS:** AO2 3 marks. Award 1 mark for identifying a disadvantage, 1 for development and 1 for context. For example, workers may be made redundant because private firms seek to reduce costs in the telecoms industry in Angola to increase efficiency. Accept other appropriate responses.

**1(i) — 6 marks.** Figure 2 shows an economy’s PPC: at A, 160 million consumer goods and 350 million capital goods; at B, 130 million consumer goods and 450 million capital goods. With reference to the data and your knowledge of economics, analyse the impact on the economy of moving from A to B.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q1 PDF pages 2–6; prompt match p. 5 (prompt-prefix-3-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.5; Figure 2 labels both combinations on the PPC.

- **MS:** AO2 (3 marks)/AO3 (3 marks). A PPC shows combinations of goods producible with available resources. At A, 350 million capital goods and 160 million consumer goods are produced; moving to B raises capital goods to 450 million but reduces consumer goods by 30 million to 130 million (opportunity cost). Increased capital-goods production can expand productive capacity, shift the PPC outwards and allow more capital and consumer goods in future; this is positive economic growth. Level guidance: 1–2 basic, limited application/analysis; 3–4 partial application and some analysis; 5–6 clear, appropriate application and thorough analysis.

**2(a) — 1 marks.** Figure 3 shows quantity demanded increasing by 4.6% while income increases by 2%. Which option best describes demand for the product? A Unitary; B Luxury; C Inferior; D Inelastic.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q2 PDF pages 7–11
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.7; the table gives the two percentage changes used to calculate YED.

- **MS:** AO2 1 mark. The only correct answer is B, Luxury. The YED is 4.6% ÷ 2% = 2.3, greater than 1.

**2(b) — 1 marks.** Which one of the following is the formula for social benefits? A Private costs + private benefits; B Private benefits − private costs; C Private benefits + external benefits; D Private costs − external costs.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q2 PDF pages 7–11; prompt match p. 7 (prompt-prefix-8-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.7.

- **MS:** AO1 1 mark. The only correct answer is C, private benefits + external benefits.

**2(c) — 2 marks.** What is meant by the term trade union?
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q2 PDF pages 7–11; prompt match p. 8 (prompt-prefix-8-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.8.

- **MS:** AO1 2 marks. Award 1 mark for an organisation for employees and 1 for aiming to protect members’ interests. Accept any other appropriate response.

**2(d) — 2 marks.** Figure 4 gives monthly costs for a firm making 3,000 units: total fixed costs $6,200 and total variable costs $3,550. Calculate average cost per unit (AC) and show your working.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q2 PDF pages 7–11
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.8; the table gives quantity and both total-cost components.

- **MS:** AO2 2 marks. Correct calculation: ($6,200 + $3,550) ÷ 3,000 = $3.25 average cost per unit. Award one mark for correct working and one for the answer; award full marks for $3.25 without working. Do not award marks for a formula alone.

**2(e) — 2 marks.** Describe one way that high start-up costs can be a barrier to entry.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q2 PDF pages 7–11; prompt match p. 9 (prompt-prefix-12-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.9.

- **MS:** AO1 2 marks. Award 1 mark for a way and 1 for development. For example, a large amount of money may be needed to set up a business, so potential competitors may be unable to raise or borrow enough. Accept other appropriate responses.

**2(f) — 3 marks.** UK supermarkets operate in an oligopoly where the market is dominated by several large firms. Explain one disadvantage of this market structure for UK consumers.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q2 PDF pages 7–11; prompt match p. 9 (prompt-prefix-12-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.9; the context identifies the market and its structure.

- **MS:** AO2 3 marks. Award 1 mark for a relevant disadvantage, 1 for development and 1 for context. For example, oligopoly firms may collude, so supermarkets may fix grocery prices and consumers may have to pay higher prices. Accept other appropriate responses.

**2(g) — 9 marks.** Foxconn Technology produces Apple’s new iPhone at its Shenzhen factory in China, employing 270,000 workers, each performing a specific task from screen handling and assembly to checking and packaging. With reference to the data and your knowledge of economics, assess the benefits to Foxconn Technology of using division of labour.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q2 PDF pages 7–11; prompt match p. 10 (prompt-prefix-3-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.10; workforce size and examples of specialised production tasks are the application context.

- **MS:** AO2 (3 marks)/AO3 (3 marks)/AO4 (3 marks). AO2/AO3: division of labour breaks production into smaller tasks; each worker specialises (for example handling the screen, checking the phone or packaging it); specialisation can make work faster and more accurate, increasing productivity and reducing Foxconn’s production costs. Workers avoid time lost switching tasks. AO4: repetitive iPhone tasks may bore workers and reduce motivation, causing mistakes, rework, lower productivity and higher costs. A failure at one production stage can halt later stages; this risk may matter with 270,000 workers and many divided tasks. Specialisation may also create problems when a worker is absent and no replacement has the required skill. Level guidance: 1–3 basic/limited and one-sided; 4–6 partial analysis with more than one view but potentially unbalanced; 7–9 clear applied analysis and a balanced, coherent evaluation.

**3(a) — 1 marks.** Which one of the following is the definition of a merger? A The joining together of two firms; B The transfer of ownership from government; C The takeover of one firm by another; D The creation of a competitive market.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q3 PDF pages 12–16; prompt match p. 12 (prompt-prefix-8-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.12.

- **MS:** AO1 1 mark. The only correct answer is A, the joining together of two firms.

**3(b) — 1 marks.** Which one of the following is a reason why a government would introduce a minimum wage? A To increase benefits paid by government; B To reduce worker motivation; C To close the income gap between rich and poor; D To ensure producers increase profits.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q3 PDF pages 12–16; prompt match p. 12 (prompt-prefix-12-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.12.

- **MS:** AO1 1 mark. The only correct answer is C, to close the income gap between the rich and the poor.

**3(c) — 3 marks.** On the blank pie chart, draw and label the likely approximate employment shares in the primary (P), secondary (S) and tertiary (T) sectors for a developed country such as Japan.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q3 PDF pages 12–16; prompt match p. 12 (prompt-prefix-5-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.12; Figure 5 is a blank pie chart.

- **MS:** AO2 3 marks. Award 1 mark for the tertiary sector being largest, 1 for secondary being second largest and 1 for primary being smallest. The mark-scheme illustration gives T = 55%, S = 25%, P = 20%.

**3(d) — 6 marks.** A $296 million residential tower is planned for Sydney’s business district, although some local people oppose it. With reference to the data and your knowledge of economics, analyse possible external costs during construction of the tower.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q3 PDF pages 12–16
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.13; construction in a busy business district and local opposition provide context.

- **MS:** AO2 (3 marks)/AO3 (3 marks). External costs are negative impacts on third parties. Local people and workers in Sydney’s business district could experience noise and air pollution, congestion or overcrowding during construction of the $296m residential tower. Construction may create noise and dust and require many vehicles; the affected third parties are neither the producers nor consumers. Level guidance: 1–2 basic/limited; 3–4 partial application and some analysis; 5–6 clear application and thorough analysis.

**3(e) — 9 marks.** Dubai has a population of over 2.5 million; immigrant workers make up over 80% of the population, many in construction, cleaning or care. With reference to the data and your knowledge of economics, assess the benefits to Dubai of employing such a high proportion of migrant labour.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q3 PDF pages 12–16
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.15; population share and occupations are the application context.

- **MS:** AO2 (3 marks)/AO3 (3 marks)/AO4 (3 marks). AO2/AO3: migration is movement to live in a foreign country; migrant workers increase labour supply, potentially shifting labour supply right, increasing employment and reducing shortages. Dubai’s 2 million migrants (80% of the population), including construction workers, make a large contribution to labour supply and may support productivity and economic expansion. AO4: most migrants work in construction, cleaning and care, so other sectors may still lack workers; migrants may not have the required skills and supply may be short-term if they return home. Raising the retirement age, lowering the school-leaving age or enabling more women to work may offer a more effective long-term or cross-sector labour supply. Level guidance: 1–3 basic/limited and one-sided; 4–6 partial analysis and potentially unbalanced; 7–9 clear applied analysis and balanced evaluation.

**4(a) — 2 marks.** Figure 6 shows two products: X has price $3,000, quantity sold 50 and PED −0.62; Y has price $3,300, quantity sold 40 and PED −1.53. Calculate total revenue for the product with inelastic PED and show your working.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q4 PDF pages 17–22; prompt match p. 17 (prompt-prefix-3-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.17; the table gives price, quantity and PED for products X and Y.

- **MS:** AO2 2 marks. Select product X (inelastic PED); total revenue = $3,000 × 50 = $150,000. Award one mark for selecting X and one for correct revenue; award both for $150,000 without workings.

**4(b) — 6 marks.** In Poland, obesity has increased. Consumers of sugary drinks such as Coca-Cola and Fanta pay an additional 0.50 zloty ($0.11) per litre after a sugar tax; advertising has also increased, and consumers continue buying the drinks despite the higher price. With reference to the data and your knowledge of economics, analyse why consumers may not maximise their benefit when consuming sugary drinks.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q4 PDF pages 17–22
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.18; the sugar tax, advertising and persistent purchases provide context.

- **MS:** AO2 (3 marks)/AO3 (3 marks). Consumers may not calculate what is best for them; increased advertising can pressure them to buy unhealthy products. Consumers may lack full information about the negative effects of sugar in drinks such as Coca Cola and Fanta. Habits and taste can make behaviour difficult to change; consumers may continue buying despite the extra 0.50 zloty ($0.11) per litre, especially if it is a small share of income. Friends’ consumption may also influence consumers to copy the behaviour. Level guidance: 1–2 basic/limited; 3–4 partial application and some analysis; 5–6 clear application and thorough analysis.

**4(c) — 12 marks.** NT Massage is a successful family-run Hong Kong business serving long-standing local customers. Mei wants to expand into a chain in busier areas, train employees, advertise in hotels and attract tourists and business people among Hong Kong’s 7.5 million population; many similar businesses compete. With reference to the data and your knowledge of economics, evaluate whether the family should expand rather than keep the business small.
- **Source locator (navigation only):** QP Q4 PDF pages 17–22; prompt match p. 20 (prompt-prefix-3-words)
- **Context locator:** Manually transcribed from visually reviewed local/supplied QP; QP PDF p.20; existing clientele, proposed expansion, target market, pricing opportunity and competitors carry the context.

- **MS:** AO2 (4 marks)/AO3 (4 marks)/AO4 (4 marks). AO2/AO3: expansion may serve more customers and increase profit; NT Massage could charge higher prices in busier locations, attract tourists and business people, increase revenue, spread risk beyond its current residential location, gain access to finance and benefit from economies of scale such as spreading advertising costs. AO4: expansion could weaken the traditional service valued by long-standing customers; coordinating several shops may create diseconomies of scale for a family used to one shop; competitors may make custom difficult to attract; growth takes time and money, delays profit and may strain the existing business. The judgement may depend on competitors, successful advertising and tourism/business demand. Level guidance: 1–4 isolated/basic, limited development, one-sided and unlikely supported judgement; 5–8 some selectivity and development, more than one view but balance/support may be limited; 9–12 specific, accurate, coherent applied analysis, balanced evaluation and supported judgement.
